## ![](_page_0_Picture_2.jpeg)

# **Wisconsin**

**Wisconsin** [\(/w](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English)[ɪˈ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English)[sk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English)[ɒ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English)[ns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English)[ɪ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English)[n/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English) [ⓘ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:En-us-Wisconsin.ogg) *[wih-SKON-sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key)*) [\[11\]](#page-15-6) is a [state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U. S._state) in the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes_region) region of the [Upper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Midwest) [Midwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Midwest) of the United States. It borders [Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota) to the west, [Iowa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iowa) to the southwest, [Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois) to the south, [Lake Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Michigan) to the east, [Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan) to the northeast, and [Lake Superior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Superior) to the north. With a population of about 6 million[\[12\]](#page-15-7) and an area of about 65,500 square miles, Wisconsin is the [20th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U. S._states_and_territories_by_population)[largest state by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U. S._states_and_territories_by_population) and the [23rd-largest by area.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U. S._states_and_territories_by_area) It has [72 counties.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_Wisconsin) Its [most populous city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_Wisconsin_by_population) is [Milwaukee;](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee) its [capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_capitals_in_the_United_States) and second-most populous city is [Madison.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison,_Wisconsin) Other urban areas include [Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Bay,_Wisconsin) [Bay,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Bay,_Wisconsin) [Kenosha,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenosha,_Wisconsin) [Racine,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racine,_Wisconsin) [Eau Claire,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eau_Claire,_Wisconsin) and the [Fox Cities.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_Cities)[\[13\]](#page-16-0)

[Wisconsin's geography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Wisconsin) is diverse, shaped by [Ice Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_glacial_period) glaciers except in the [Driftless Area.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driftless_Area) The [Northern Highland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Highland) and [Western Upland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Upland) along with a part of the [Central Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Plain_(Wisconsin)) occupy the state's western part, with lowlands stretching to Lake Michigan. Wisconsin is third to [Ontario](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ontario) and Michigan in the length of its [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes) coastline. Its northern portion is home to the [Chequamegon–Nicolet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chequamegon%E2%80%93Nicolet_National_Forest) [National Forest.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chequamegon%E2%80%93Nicolet_National_Forest) At the time of European contact, the area was inhabited by [Algonquian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algonquian_peoples) and [Siouan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siouan) nations, and today it is home to [eleven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_federally_recognized_tribes_by_state#Wisconsin) federally recognized [tribes.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribe_(Native_American))[\[14\]](#page-16-1) Originally part of the [Northwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Territory) [Territory,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Territory) it was [admitted as a state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admission_to_the_Union) in 1848. During the 19th and early 20th centuries, many European settlers entered the state, mostly from [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germany) and [Scandinavia.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavia)[\[15\]](#page-16-2)[\[16\]](#page-16-3) Wisconsin remains a center of [German American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_American) and [Scandinavian American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavian_American) culture[,\[17\]](#page-16-4) particularly in respect to its [cuisine,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuisine_of_Wisconsin) with foods such as [bratwurst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bratwurst) and [kringle.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kringle)

Wisconsin is one of the nation's leading [dairy producers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dairy_farming) and is known as "America's Dairyland"; it is particularly famous for [its cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_cheese)[.\[18\]](#page-16-5)[\[19\]](#page-16-6) The state is also famous for [its beer,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_breweries_in_Wisconsin) particularly and historically [in Milwaukee,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_in_Milwaukee) most notably as the headquarters of the [Miller Brewing Company.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miller_Brewing_Company) Wisconsin has some of the nation's most permissive [alcohol laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_laws_of_Wisconsin) and is known for its [drinking culture.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drinking_culture) [\[20\]](#page-16-7)[\[21\]](#page-16-8) Its economy is dominated by manufacturing, healthcare, information technology, and agriculture—specifically dairy, [cranberries,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cranberries) and [ginseng.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ginseng)[\[22\]](#page-16-9) Tourism is also a major contributor to its economy[.\[23\]](#page-16-10) The [gross domestic product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_states_and_territories_of_the_United_States_by_GDP) in 2020 was \$348 billion.[\[24\]](#page-16-11) Wisconsin is home to one [UNESCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO) [World Heritage Site,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Site) comprising [two of the most signi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_20th-Century_Architecture_of_Frank_Lloyd_Wright)ficant buildings designed by Wisconsin-born architect [Frank Lloyd Wright:](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Lloyd_Wright) his studio at [Taliesin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliesin_(studio)) near [Spring Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_Green,_Wisconsin) and his [Jacobs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_and_Katherine_Jacobs_First_House) [I House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_and_Katherine_Jacobs_First_House) in Madison[.\[25\]](#page-16-12) The [Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States)) was founded in Wisconsin in 1854; in modern elections, it is considered a [swing state.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swing_state)

## **Etymology**

The word *Wisconsin* originates from the name given to the [Wisconsin River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_River) by one of the [Algonquian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algonquian_languages)speaking Native American groups living in the region at the time of [European colonization.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_colonization_of_the_Americas)[\[26\]](#page-16-13) The French explorer [Jacques Marquette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Marquette) was the first European to reach the Wisconsin River, arriving in 1673 and calling the river *Meskousing* (likely ᒣᔅᑯᐤᓯᣙ *meskowsin*) in his journal.[\[27\]](#page-16-14) Subsequent French writers changed the spelling from *Meskousing* to *Ouisconsin*, and over time this became the name for both the Wisconsin River and the surrounding lands. English speakers [anglicized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicized) the spelling from *Ouisconsin* to *Wisconsin* when they began to arrive in large numbers during the early 19th century. The legislature of [Wisconsin Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Territory) made the current spelling official in 1845[.\[28\]](#page-16-15)

The [Algonquian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algonquian_languages) word for Wisconsin and its original meaning have both grown obscure. While interpretations vary, most implicate the river and the red sandstone that lines its banks. One leading theory holds that the name originated from the [Miami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami-Illinois_language) word *Meskonsing*, meaning 'it lies red', a reference to the setting of the Wisconsin River as it flows through the reddish sandstone of the [Wisconsin Dells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dells_of_the_Wisconsin_River)[.\[29\]](#page-16-16) Other theories include claims that the name originated from one of a variety of [Ojibwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ojibwa) words meaning 'red stone place', 'where the waters gather', or 'great rock'.[\[30\]](#page-16-17)

## **History**

#### **Early history**

Wisconsin has been home to a wide variety of cultures over the past 14,000 years. The first people arrived around 10,000 BCE during the [Wisconsin Glaciation.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Glaciation) These early inhabitants, called [Paleo-](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleo-Indians)

finally established an American presence in the area.[\[42\]](#page-17-11) Under American control, the economy of the territory shifted from fur trading to lead mining. The prospect of easy mineral wealth drew immigrants from throughout the U. S. and Europe to the lead deposits at [Mineral Point,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineral_Point,_Wisconsin) [Dodgeville,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dodgeville,_Wisconsin) and nearby areas. Some miners found shelter in the holes they had dug, and earned the nickname "badgers", leading to Wisconsin's identity as the "Badger State".[\[43\]](#page-17-12) The sudden influx of white miners prompted tension with the local Native American population. The [Winnebago War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winnebago_War) of 1827 and the [Black Hawk War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hawk_War) of 1832 culminated in the forced [removal of Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_removal) from most parts of the state.[\[44\]](#page-17-13)

Following these conflicts, [Wisconsin Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Territory) was created by an act of the [United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Congress) on April 20, 1836. By fall of that year, the best prairie groves of the counties surrounding what is now Milwaukee were occupied by farmers from the [New England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England) states[.\[45\]](#page-17-14)

[Jean Nicolet,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Nicolet) depicted in a 1910 painting by Frank Rohrbeck, was probably the first European to explore Wisconsin. The mural is located in the [Brown County Courthouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_County_Courthouse_(Wisconsin)) in Green Bay.

#### **Statehood**

The [Erie Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erie_Canal) facilitated the travel of both [Yankee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yankee) settlers and European immigrants to Wisconsin Territory. Yankees from New England and [upstate New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upstate_New_York) seized a dominant position in law and politics, enacting policies that marginalized the region's earlier Native American and French-Canadian residents.[\[46\]](#page-17-15) Yankees also speculated in real estate, platted towns such as Racine, Beloit, Burlington, and Janesville, and established schools, civic institutions, and [Congregationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregationalist) churches.[\[47\]](#page-17-16)[\[48\]](#page-17-17)[\[49\]](#page-17-18) At the same time, many [Germans,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germans) Irish, [Norwegians,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegians) and other immigrants also settled in towns and farms across the territory, establishing [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic) and [Lutheran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutheran) institutions.

The growing population allowed Wisconsin to gain statehood on May 29, 1848, as the 30th state. Between 1840 and 1850, Wisconsin's non-Indian population had swollen from 31,000 to 305,000. More than a third of residents (110,500) were foreign born, including 38,000 Germans, 28,000 British immigrants from England, Scotland, and Wales, and 21,000 Irish. Another third (103,000) were Yankees from New England and western New York state. Only about 63,000 residents in 1850 had been born in Wisconsin[.\[50\]](#page-17-19)

[Nelson Dewey,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Dewey) the first [governor of Wisconsin,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Wisconsin) was a [Democrat.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Democratic_Party_(United_States)) Dewey oversaw the transition from the territorial to the new state government[.\[51\]](#page-17-20) He encouraged the development of the state's infrastructure, particularly the construction of new roads, railroads, canals, and harbors, as well as the improvement of the [Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_River_(Illinois_River_tributary)) and [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_River) [Rivers.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_River)[\[51\]](#page-17-20) During his administration, the [State Board of Public Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Wisconsin_Board_of_Public_Works&action=edit&redlink=1) was organized[.\[51\]](#page-17-20) Dewey, an [abolitionist,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionist) was the first of many Wisconsin governors to advocate against the spread of [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States) into new states and territories. [\[51\]](#page-17-20)

The [Francois Vertefeuille House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francois_Vertefeuille_House) in [Prairie du Chien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prairie_du_Chien,_Wisconsin) was built in the 1810s by fur traders.

## ![](_page_2_Picture_12.jpeg)

On May 29, 1948, the U. S. Post Office issued a [commemorative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commemorative_stamp) [stamp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commemorative_stamp) celebrating the 100th anniversary of Wisconsin statehood, featuring the state capitol building and map of Wisconsin.

#### **Civil War**

## ![](_page_2_Picture_15.jpeg)

The [Little White Schoolhouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_White_Schoolhouse) in [Ripon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ripon,_Wisconsin) held the nation's first meeting of the [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States)) [Party.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States))

Politics in early Wisconsin were defined by the greater national debate over slavery. A free state from its foundation, Wisconsin became a center of northern [abolitionism.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States) The debate became especially intense in 1854 after [Joshua Glover,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua_Glover) a runaway slave from [Missouri,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri) was captured in [Racine.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racine,_Wisconsin)

Glover was taken into custody under the Federal [Fugitive Slave Law,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fugitive_Slave_Act_of_1850) but a mob of abolitionists stormed the prison where Glover was held and helped him escape to Canada. In a trial stemming from the incident, the [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Supreme_Court) [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Supreme_Court) ultimately declared the Fugitive Slave Law unconstitutional.[\[52\]](#page-17-21) The [Republican Party,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States)) founded on March 20, 1854, by anti-slavery expansion activists in [Ripon, Wisconsin,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ripon,_Wisconsin) grew to dominate state politics in the aftermath of these events.[\[53\]](#page-17-22)

During the [American Civil War,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) around 91,000 troops from Wisconsin fought for the [Union.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_(American_Civil_War))[\[54\]](#page-17-23) A number of Wisconsin regiments were distinguished, including three that served in the celebrated ["Iron Brigade"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Brigade)— the [2nd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_Wisconsin_Infantry_Regiment) [Wisconsin,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_Wisconsin_Infantry_Regiment) [6th Wisconsin,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6th_Wisconsin_Infantry_Regiment) and [7th Wisconsin.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7th_Wisconsin_Infantry_Regiment) The [8th Wisconsin,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8th_Wisconsin_Infantry_Regiment) another hard-fighting regiment, was often accompanied into battle by its mascot, [Old Abe,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Abe) a bald eagle.

#### **Economic progress**

Wisconsin's economy also diversified during the early years of statehood. While lead mining diminished, agriculture became a principal occupation in the southern half of the state. Railroads were built across the state to help transport grains to market, and industries like [J. I. Case & Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_Corporation) in Racine were founded to build agricultural equipment. Wisconsin briefly became one of the nation's leading producers of wheat during the 1860s.[\[55\]](#page-17-24) Meanwhile, the lumber industry dominated in the heavily forested northern sections of Wisconsin, and sawmills sprang up in cities like [La Crosse,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Crosse) [Eau Claire,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eau_Claire,_Wisconsin) and [Wausau.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wausau,_Wisconsin) These economic activities had dire environmental consequences. By the close of the 19th century, intensive agriculture had devastated soil fertility, and lumbering had deforested most of the state[.\[56\]](#page-17-25) These conditions forced both wheat agriculture and the lumber industry into a precipitous decline.

Beginning in the 1890s, farmers in Wisconsin shifted from wheat to dairy production to make more sustainable and profitable use of their land. Many immigrants carried cheese-making traditions that, combined with the state's suitable geography and dairy research led by [Stephen Babcock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Babcock) at the [University of Wisconsin,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Wisconsin) helped the state build a reputation as "America's Dairyland".[\[57\]](#page-17-26) Meanwhile, conservationists including [Aldo Leopold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aldo_Leopold) helped reestablish the state's forests during the early 20th century[,\[58\]](#page-18-0) paving the way for a more renewable lumber and [paper milling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paper_mill) industry as well as promoting recreational tourism in the northern woodlands. Manufacturing also boomed in Wisconsin during the early 20th century, driven by an immense immigrant workforce arriving from Europe. Industries in cities like Milwaukee ranged from brewing and food processing to heavy machine production and tool-making, leading Wisconsin to rank 8th among U. S. states in total product value by 1910.[\[59\]](#page-18-2)

#### **20th century**

The early 20th century was also notable for the emergence of [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism_in_the_United_States) politics championed by [Robert M. La](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_M._La_Follette) [Follette.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_M._La_Follette) Between 1901 and 1914, Progressive Republicans in Wisconsin created the nation's first comprehensive statewide [primary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partisan_primary) system[,\[61\]](#page-18-3) the first effective [workplace injury compensation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers%27_compensation) law[,\[62\]](#page-18-4) and the first state [income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax)[,\[63\]](#page-18-5) making taxation proportional to actual earnings.

During [World War I,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I) due to the neutrality of Wisconsin and many [Wisconsin Republicans,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_of_Wisconsin) [progressives,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism_in_the_United_States) and [German immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Americans) which made up 30 to 40 percent of the state population, Wisconsin would gain the nickname "Traitor State" which was used by many "hyper patriots"[.\[64\]](#page-18-6)[\[65\]](#page-18-7)[\[66\]](#page-18-8)[\[67\]](#page-18-9)

As the war raged on in Europe, [Robert M. La Follette,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_M._La_Follette) leader of the anti-war movement in Wisconsin, led a group of progressive senators in blocking a bill by president [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson) which would have armed merchant ships with guns. Many Wisconsin politicians such as [Emanuel L. Philipp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emanuel_L._Philipp) and [Irvine Lenroot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irvine_Lenroot) were accused of having divided loyalties.[\[68\]](#page-18-10) Even with outspoken opponents to the war, at the onset of the war many Wisconsinites would abandon neutrality. Businesses, labor and farms all enjoyed prosperity from the war. With over 118,000 going into military service, Wisconsin was the first state to report for the national drafts conducted by the [U. S. military.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Armed_Forces)[\[69\]](#page-18-11)

The progressive [Wisconsin Idea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Idea) also promoted the statewide expansion of the University of Wisconsin through the [UW-Extension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UW-Extension) system at this time[.\[70\]](#page-18-12) Later, UW economics professors [John R. Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_R._Commons) and Harold Groves helped Wisconsin create the first [unemployment compensation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment_compensation) program in the United States in 1932.[\[71\]](#page-18-13) Other [Wisconsin Idea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Idea) scholars at the university generated the plan that became the New Deal's [Social Security Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Security_Act) of 1935, with Wisconsin expert [Arthur J. Altmeyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_J._Altmeyer) playing the key role[.\[72\]](#page-18-14)

In the immediate aftermath of [World War II,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II) citizens of Wisconsin were divided over issues such as creation of the [United Nations,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations) support for the European recovery, and the growth of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union)'s power. However, when Europe divided into Communist and capitalist camps and the [Chinese Communist Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Revolution) succeeded in 1949, public opinion began to move towards support for the protection of democracy and capitalism against Communist expansion[.\[73\]](#page-18-15)

Wisconsin took part in several political extremes in the mid to late 20th century, ranging from the [anti-communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mc Carthyism) crusades of Senator [Joseph Mc Carthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Mc Carthy) in the 1950s to the radical antiwar protests at UW-Madison that culminated in the [Sterling Hall bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sterling_Hall_bombing) in August 1970. The state undertook [welfare reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_reform) under Republican Governor [Tommy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommy_Thompson) [Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommy_Thompson) during the 1990s.[\[74\]](#page-18-16) The state's economy also underwent further transformations towards the close of the 20th century, as heavy industry and manufacturing declined in favor of a [service economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_economy) based on medicine, education, agribusiness, and tourism.

## ![](_page_3_Picture_10.jpeg)

The [Daniel E. Krause Stone Barn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_E._Krause_Stone_Barn) in [Chase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chase,_Wisconsin) was built in 1903, as dairy farming spread across the state.

## ![](_page_3_Picture_12.jpeg)

Suff[ragists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women%27s_suffrage_in_Wisconsin) campaigning, 1916. Wisconsin was among the earliest states to ratify the [Nineteenth Amendment.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nineteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution)[\[60\]](#page-18-1)

## ![](_page_3_Picture_14.jpeg)

In the 20th century, [Superior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superior,_Wisconsin) was a major hub for shipping iron ore across the Great Lakes via [lake freighters.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_freighter)

#### **21st century**

In 2011, Wisconsin became the focus of some controversy when newly elected governor [Scott Walker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Walker_(politician)) proposed and then successfully passed and enacted [2011 Wisconsin Act 10,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Wisconsin_Act_10) which made large changes in the areas of collective bargaining, compensation, retirement, health insurance, and sick leave of public sector employees, among other changes.[\[75\]](#page-18-17) A [series of major protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Wisconsin_protests) by union supporters took place that year in protest to the changes, and Walker survived [a recall election held the next year,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_gubernatorial_recall_election) becoming the first governor in United States history to do so[.\[76\]](#page-18-18)

Since 2023, fi[ve University of Wisconsin branch campuses have closed,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closures_of_the_University_of_Wisconsin_branch_campuses) one has gone entirely online, and one has stopped using several of its buildings. Many have expressed concerns about the future of the Wisconsin Idea following these closures.[\[77\]](#page-18-19)[\[78\]](#page-18-20)[\[79\]](#page-18-21)

## **Geography**

Wisconsin is in the [Midwestern United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States) and is part of both the [Great Lakes region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes_region) and the [Upper Midwest.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Midwest) The state has a total area of 65,496 square miles (169,630 km2). Wisconsin is bordered by the [Montreal River;](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montreal_River_(Wisconsin%E2%80%93Michigan)) [Lake Superior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Superior) and [Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan) to the north; by [Lake Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Michigan) to the east; by [Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois) to the south; and by [Iowa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iowa) to the southwest and [Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota) to the northwest. A border dispute with Michigan was settled by two cases, both [Wisconsin v.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_v._Michigan) [Michigan,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_v._Michigan) in 1934 and 1935. The state's boundaries include the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) and [St. Croix River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Croix_River_(Wisconsin%E2%80%93Minnesota)) in the west, and the [Menominee River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menominee_River) in the northeast.[\[80\]](#page-19-0)

Lying between the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes) and the Mississippi River, Wisconsin has a wide variety of geographical features. The state is divided into five distinct regions. In the north, the [Lake Superior Lowland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Superior_Lowland) occupies a belt of land along Lake Superior. Just to the south, the [Northern Highland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Highland) has massive mixed hardwood and coniferous forests including the 1,500,000-acre (610,000-hectare) [Chequamegon–Nicolet National Forest,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chequamegon%E2%80%93Nicolet_National_Forest) as well as thousands of glacial lakes, and the state's highest point, [Timms Hill.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timms_Hill) In the middle of the state, the [Central Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Plain_(Wisconsin)) has some unique [sandstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandstone) formations like the [Dells of the](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dells_of_the_Wisconsin_River)

[Wisconsin River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dells_of_the_Wisconsin_River) in addition to rich farmland. The [Eastern Ridges and Lowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Ridges_and_Lowlands) region in the southeast is home to many of Wisconsin's largest cities. The ridges include the [Niagara Escarpment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niagara_Escarpment) that stretches from New York, the [Black River Escarpment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_River_Escarpment_(Wisconsin)) and the [Magnesian Escarpment.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnesian_Escarpment)[\[81\]](#page-19-1)[\[82\]](#page-19-2)[\[83\]](#page-19-3) In the southwest, the [Western Upland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Upland) is a rugged landscape with a mix of forest and farmland, including many bluffs on the Mississippi River, and the [Baraboo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baraboo_Range) [Range.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baraboo_Range) This region is part of the [Driftless Area,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driftless_Area) which also includes parts of Iowa, Illinois, and Minnesota. Overall, 46% of Wisconsin's land area is covered by forest.

Wisconsin has geologic formations and deposits that vary in age from over three billion years to several thousand years, with most rocks being millions of years old[.\[84\]](#page-19-4) The oldest geologic formations were created over 600 million years ago during the [Precambrian,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precambrian) the majority below the glacial deposits. Much of the Baraboo Range consists of [Baraboo Quartzite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baraboo_Quartzite) and other Precambrian [metamorphic rock.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metamorphic_rock)[\[85\]](#page-19-5)[\[86\]](#page-19-6) This area was not covered by [glaciers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glacier) during the most recent ice age, the [Wisconsin Glaciation.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Glaciation) [Langlade County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Langlade_County) has a soil rarely found outside the county called [Antigo silt loam.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antigo_(soil))[\[87\]](#page-19-7)

## ![](_page_4_Picture_4.jpeg)

## Wisconsin geographic regions

The state has more than 12,000 named rivers and streams, totaling 84,000 miles (135,000 km) in length.[\[88\]](#page-19-8) It has over 15,000 named lakes, totaling about 1 million acres (4,000 km2). [Lake Winnebago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Winnebago) is the largest inland lake, with over 137,700 acres (557 km2), and 88 miles (142 km) of shoreline. Along the two Great Lakes, Wisconsin has over 500 miles (800 km) of shoreline[.\[89\]](#page-19-9) Many of the [named islands in Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_Wisconsin) are in the Great Lakes; many surround the [Door Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Door_Peninsula) in Lake Michigan or are part of the [Apostle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostle_Islands) [Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostle_Islands) in Lake Superior[.\[90\]](#page-19-10) The Mississippi River and inland lakes and rivers contain the rest of Wisconsin's islands.

Areas under the protection of the [National Park Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Park_Service) include the [Apostle Islands National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostle_Islands_National_Lakeshore) [Lakeshore,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostle_Islands_National_Lakeshore) [Ice Age National Scenic Trail,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_Age_Trail) and portions of the [Saint Croix National Scenic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Croix_National_Scenic_Riverway) [Riverway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Croix_National_Scenic_Riverway) and [North Country National Scenic Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Country_Trail)[.\[91\]](#page-19-11) There are an additional 18 [National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_National_Natural_Landmarks_in_Wisconsin) [Natural Landmarks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_National_Natural_Landmarks_in_Wisconsin) in the state that include dune and swales, swamps, bogs, and old-growth

## ![](_page_4_Picture_8.jpeg)

The [Driftless Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driftless_Area) is characterized by bluffs carved in [sedimentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedimentary) rock by water from melting [Ice age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_age) glaciers.

## ![](_page_4_Picture_10.jpeg)

[Sea caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_caves) are located on the shorelines of the [Apostle Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostle_Islands) in [Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Superior) [Superior.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Superior)

forests. Wisconsin has [50 state park units,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Wisconsin_state_parks) covering more than 60,570 acres (245.1 km2) in state parks and state recreation areas maintained by the [Wisconsin Department of Natural Resources.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Department_of_Natural_Resources) The Division of Forestry manages a further 471,329 acres (1,907.40 km2) in [Wisconsin's state forests.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Wisconsin_state_forests)[\[92\]](#page-19-12)

#### **Climate**

Most of Wisconsin is classified as warm-summer [humid continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate) [\(Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) *Dfb*), while southern and southwestern portions are classified as hot-summer humid continental climate (Köppen *Dfa*). The highest temperature ever recorded in the state was in the Wisconsin Dells, on July 13, 1936, where it reached 114 °F (46 °C). The lowest temperature ever recorded in Wisconsin was in the village of [Couderay,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Couderay) where it reached −55 °F (−48 °C) on both February 2 and 4, 1996. Wisconsin also receives a large amount of regular snowfall averaging around 40 inches (100 cm) in the southern portions with up to 160 inches (410 cm) annually in the Lake Superior [snowbelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snowbelt) each year[.\[93\]](#page-19-13)

#### **Cities**

Wisconsin has three types of municipality: cities, villages, and towns. [Cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_(Wisconsin)) and [villages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Village_(Wisconsin)) are incorporated urban areas. [Towns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Town_(Wisconsin)) are unincorporated [minor civil divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_civil_division) of [counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_subdivisions_of_Wisconsin#County) with limited self-government.

Over two-thirds of Wisconsin residents live in urban areas.[\[95\]](#page-19-15) [Milwaukee,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee) in southeastern Wisconsin, is the state's most populous city, with approximately 580,000 people. The [Milwaukee metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_metropolitan_area) accounts for 1.57 million of the state's residents. With a population of nearly 280,000, the state capital of [Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison,_Wisconsin) is consistently ranked as one of the most livable cities in both the state and country and is the [fastest-growing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_growth) city in Wisconsin[.\[96\]](#page-19-16)[\[97\]](#page-19-17) The [Madison metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison_metropolitan_area) in southern Wisconsin has about 680,000 residents.

Medium-size cities dot the state. The largest of these is [Green Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Bay,_Wisconsin) in northeastern Wisconsin, with approximately 320,000 people in the metro area. Other metropolitan cities in the state include [Appleton,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appleton,_Wisconsin) [Racine,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racine,_Wisconsin) [Oshkosh,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oshkosh,_Wisconsin) [Eau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eau_Claire,_Wisconsin) [Claire,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eau_Claire,_Wisconsin) [Janesville,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janesville,_Wisconsin) [Wausau,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wausau,_Wisconsin) [La Crosse,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Crosse,_Wisconsin) [Sheboygan,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheboygan,_Wisconsin) and [Fond du Lac.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fond_du_Lac,_Wisconsin) Furthermore, another 12 cities function as centers of [micropolitan statistical areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micropolitan_statistical_areas) which typically anchor a network of working farms surrounding them. As of 2011, there were 12 cities in Wisconsin with a population of 50,000 or more, accounting for 73% of the state's employment[.\[98\]](#page-19-18)

## **Demographics**

#### **Population**

The [United States Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) estimates that the population of Wisconsin was 5,822,434 on July 1, 2019, a 2.4% increase since the [2010 United States census.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census)[\[102\]](#page-19-20) This includes a natural increase since the last census of 150,659 people (i.e., 614,771 births minus 464,112 deaths) and a decrease due to net migration of 12,755 people. Immigration resulted in a net increase of 59,251 people, and migration from within the U. S. resulted in a net decrease of 72,006 people[.\[103\]](#page-20-0)

According to [HUD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development)'s 2022 [Annual Homeless Assessment Report,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_Homeless_Assessment_Report_to_Congress) there were an estimated 4,775 [homeless](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeless)

In 2022, the five largest European ancestry groups were: [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_American) (36%), [Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_American) (10.2%), [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_American) (7.9%), [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_American) (6.7%), and [Norwegian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_American) (6.3%).[\[111\]](#page-20-8) German is the most common ancestry in every county in the state, except Menominee, Trempealeau, and Vernon[.\[112\]](#page-20-9) Wisconsin has the highest percentage of residents of Polish ancestry of any state[.\[113\]](#page-20-10) According to the 2022 [American Community Survey,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey) 7.6% of Wisconsin's population were of [Hispanic or Latino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans) origin (of any race). The largest Hispanic ancestry groups were [Mexican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_American) (5.1%), [Puerto Rican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Ricans) (1.1%), [Central American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_America) (0.4%), and [Cuban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_American) (0.1%), with 0.9% reporting other Hispanic or Latino origins[.\[114\]](#page-20-11)

Since its founding, Wisconsin has been ethnically heterogeneous. Following the period of French fur traders, the next wave of settlers were miners, many of whom were [Cornish,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornish_American) who settled the southwestern area of the state. The next wave was dominated by "Yankees", migrants of [English descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_American) from [New](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England) [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England) and [upstate New York;](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upstate_New_York) in the early years of statehood, they dominated the state's heavy industry, finance, politics, and education. Between 1850 and 1900, the immigrants were mostly [Germans,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Americans) [Scandinavians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandinavian_Americans) (the largest group being [Norwegian\)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_American), [Irish,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Americans) and [Poles.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Americans) In the 20th century, a number of African Americans and [Mexicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Americans) settled in [Milwaukee;](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee) and after the end of the [Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War) came an influx of [Hmongs.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hmong_Americans)

## Ethnic origins in Wisconsin

The various ethnic groups settled in different areas of the state. Although German immigrants settled throughout the state, the largest concentration was in Milwaukee. Norwegian immigrants settled in lumbering and farming areas in the north and west. Irish, Italian, and Polish immigrants settled primarily in urban areas.[\[115\]](#page-20-12) [Menominee County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menominee_County,_Wisconsin) is the only county in the eastern United States with a Native American majority.

African Americans came to Milwaukee, especially from 1940 on. 86% of Wisconsin's African-American population live in four cities: [Milwaukee,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee) [Racine,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racine,_Wisconsin) [Beloit,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beloit,_Wisconsin) [Kenosha,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenosha) with Milwaukee home to nearly three-fourths of the state's black Americans. In the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes) region, only Detroit and [Cleveland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland) have a higher percentage of African-American residents.[\[116\]](#page-20-13)

About 33% of Wisconsin's Asian population is [Hmong,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hmong_people) with significant communities in [Milwaukee,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee) [Wausau,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wausau,_Wisconsin) [Green Bay,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Bay,_Wisconsin) [Sheboygan,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheboygan,_Wisconsin) [Appleton,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appleton,_Wisconsin) [Madison,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison,_Wisconsin) [La Crosse,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Crosse) [Eau Claire,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eau_Claire,_Wisconsin) [Oshkosh,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oshkosh,_Wisconsin) and [Manitowoc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manitowoc,_Wisconsin)[\[117\]](#page-20-14) 61,629 people in Wisconsin, or around 1% of the population, identify as Hmong[.\[118\]](#page-20-15)

Of the residents of Wisconsin, 71.7% were born in Wisconsin, 23.0% were born in a different US state, 0.7% were born in Puerto Rico, U. S. Island areas, or born abroad to American parent(s), and 4.6% were foreign born[.\[119\]](#page-20-16)

In 2018, the countries of origin for Wisconsin's immigrants came from Mexico, India, China, [Laos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laos) and the [Philippines.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines)[\[120\]](#page-20-17)
